its Jeffrey validity. But first, we introduce the multinomial
distribution, in general, multivariate form. It captures the
probabilities associated with drawing (with replacement) multiple
balls from an urn, filled with balls of various colours. The urn is
represented by a distribution
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
, where
X
is the set
of colours. Thus, the probability of drawing a ball of colour
x\in X

is given by
\omega(x)
. We are interested in drawing multiple balls,
of a fixed size
K
. These draws are represented by a multiset

\varphi\in\mathcal{M}[K](X)
. The multinomial distribution

\mathsl{mn}[K](\omega)\in\mathcal{D}\big(\mathcal{M}[K](X)\big)
assigns
probabilities to such draws. It can be described as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsl{mn}[K](\omega)\hskip 2.0pt\coloneqq\hskip 2.0pt\mathcal{D}(\mathsl{acc})\big(\omega^{K}\big)&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{DstFunEqn}}}{{=}}}&\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{x}\in X^{K}}\,\omega^{K}(\vec{x})\,\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathsl{acc}(\vec{x}){\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle\\[10.00002pt]
&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{M}[K](X)}\,\sum_{\vec{x}\in\mathsl{acc}^{-1}(\varphi)}\,\omega^{K}(\vec{x})\,\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\varphi{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle.\\[11.99998pt]
&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{M}[K](X)}\,({\kern 1.0pt}\varphi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\prod_{x\in X}\omega(x)^{\varphi(x)}\,\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\varphi{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle.\end{array}

(18)

For instance, for
\omega=\frac{1}{2}|{\kern 1.00006pt}R{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{1}{3}|{\kern 1.00006pt}G{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{1}{6}|{\kern 1.00006pt}B{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
the multinomial distribution
of draws of size three is:

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsl{mn}[3](\omega)\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\frac{1}{8}\left|\vrule height=8.5pt,depth=4.5pt,width=0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt}3|{\kern 1.00006pt}R{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle{\kern 0.05005pt}\right>+\frac{1}{4}\left|\vrule height=8.5pt,depth=4.5pt,width=0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt}2|{\kern 1.00006pt}R{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+1|{\kern 1.00006pt}G{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle{\kern 0.05005pt}\right>+\frac{1}{6}\left|\vrule height=8.5pt,depth=4.5pt,width=0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt}1|{\kern 1.00006pt}R{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+2|{\kern 1.00006pt}G{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle{\kern 0.05005pt}\right>+\frac{1}{27}\left|\vrule height=8.5pt,depth=4.5pt,width=0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt}3|{\kern 1.00006pt}G{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle{\kern 0.05005pt}\right>\\[1.99997pt]
\qquad+\,\frac{1}{8}\left|\vrule height=8.5pt,depth=4.5pt,width=0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt}2|{\kern 1.00006pt}R{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+1|{\kern 1.00006pt}B{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle{\kern 0.05005pt}\right>+\frac{1}{6}\left|\vrule height=8.5pt,depth=4.5pt,width=0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt}1|{\kern 1.00006pt}R{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+1|{\kern 1.00006pt}G{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+1|{\kern 1.00006pt}B{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle{\kern 0.05005pt}\right>+\frac{1}{18}\left|\vrule height=8.5pt,depth=4.5pt,width=0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt}2|{\kern 1.00006pt}G{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+1|{\kern 1.00006pt}B{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle{\kern 0.05005pt}\right>\\[1.99997pt]
\qquad+\,\frac{1}{24}\left|\vrule height=8.5pt,depth=4.5pt,width=0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt}1|{\kern 1.00006pt}R{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+2|{\kern 1.00006pt}B{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle{\kern 0.05005pt}\right>+\frac{1}{36}\left|\vrule height=8.5pt,depth=4.5pt,width=0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt}1|{\kern 1.00006pt}G{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+2|{\kern 1.00006pt}B{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle{\kern 0.05005pt}\right>+\frac{1}{216}\left|\vrule height=8.5pt,depth=4.5pt,width=0.0pt{\kern 1.00006pt}3|{\kern 1.00006pt}B{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle{\kern 0.05005pt}\right>.\end{array}

Each draw, as multiset of size 3, is inside a big ket, with
the probability of that draw written before the big ket.

Lemma 4

Let
\psi\in\mathcal{M}[K](X)
be point evidence, where we identify
\psi=\sum_{x}\psi(x)|{\kern 1.00006pt}x{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
with the multiset of point predicates

\sum_{x}\psi(x)\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathbf{1}_{x}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle
.

1.

For a distribution
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}\psi&=&\mathsl{mn}[K](\omega)(\psi).\end{array}

2.

The distribution that gives the highest Jeffrey validity to the
point evidence
\psi
is
\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi)
.

Proof

1.

We use the equation
\omega\models\mathbf{1}_{x}=\omega(x)
in:

\begin{array}[b]{rcl}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}\psi&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{JeffreyValidity}}}{{=}}}&\displaystyle({\kern 1.0pt}\psi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\prod_{x\in X}\big(\omega\models\mathbf{1}_{x}\big)^{\psi(x)}\\[10.00002pt]
&=&\displaystyle({\kern 1.0pt}\psi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\prod_{x\in X}\omega(x)^{\psi(x)}\hskip 2.0pt\,\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{MultinomialEqn}}}{{=}}}\,\hskip 2.0pt\mathsl{mn}[K](\omega)(\psi).\end{array}

2.

This is a standard result, see

e.g.

[
26
, Ex. 17.5]
, or the appendix
of
[
20
]
, which one proves via the Lagrange multiplier
method (as in the appendix).

Pearl validity of the form (
17
) does not make much
sense for point evidence because it becomes zero as soon as there is
more than one point, since
\mathbf{1}_{x}\mathrel{\&}\mathbf{1}_{y}=\mathbf{0}
when

x\neq y
. However, in Section
6
, we shall see that
point evidence in the presence of channels does make sense for Pearl
validity.

For future use we show how Jeffrey validity of point evidence is
related to Kullback-Leibler divergence
\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}
. This divergence is a
standard method for measuring the difference between two
distributions. Its definition (here) uses the natural logarithm

\ln
. Explicitly, for
\omega,\rho\in\mathcal{D}(X)
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}(\omega,\rho)&\coloneqq&\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\,\omega(x)\cdot\displaystyle\ln\left(\frac{\omega(x)}{\rho(x)}\right).\end{array}

(19)

The convention is that
r\cdot\ln(r)=0
when
r=0
. This is not a metric, since KL-divergence
\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}
is not symmetric:

\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}(\omega,\rho)\neq\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}(\rho,\omega)
, in general. One does have

\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}(\omega,\rho)\geq 0
, which can be shown via Jensen’s
inequality, and
\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}(\omega,\rho)=0
if and only if
\omega=\rho
.

Lemma 5
(From
[
23
]
)

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{M}[K](X)
be a given multiset of size

K>0
. Consider two distributions
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(X)
with the
same support as
\varphi
. Then:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}\varphi\leq\omega^{\prime}\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}\varphi&\Longleftrightarrow&\mathsl{mn}[K]\big(\omega\big)(\varphi)\leq\mathsl{mn}[K]\big(\omega^{\prime}\big)(\varphi)\\[1.99997pt]
&\Longleftrightarrow&\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}\big(\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi),\,\omega\big)\geq\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}\big(\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi),\,\omega^{\prime}\big).\end{array}

The first equivalence
\Longleftrightarrow
is a direct
consequence of Lemma
4
. The second
equivalence is the main statement.

This result makes perfect sense: the lower the (Jeffrey) validity of
point evidence
\varphi
is in distribution
\omega
, the higher the
divergence is between the normalised distribution
\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)
and

\omega
. Very briefly: low validity corresponds to high divergence.

Proof

We use, as first step below, that the logarithm
\ln
preserves and
reflects the order and that it sends multiplications to sums.

\hskip-5.0pt\begin{array}[b]{rcl}\lx@intercol\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\mathsl{mn}[K]\big(\omega\big)(\varphi)\leq\mathsl{mn}[K]\big(\omega^{\prime}\big)(\varphi)$\mbox{}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[1.99997pt]
&\Longleftrightarrow&\ln\Big(\mathsl{mn}[K]\big(\omega\big)(\varphi)\Big)\leq\ln\Big(\mathsl{mn}[K]\big(\omega^{\prime}\big)(\varphi)\Big)\\[1.99997pt]
&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{MultinomialEqn}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}}&\displaystyle\ln\left(({\kern 1.0pt}\varphi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\prod_{x\in X}\,\omega(x)^{\varphi(x)}\right)\leq\ln\left(({\kern 1.0pt}\varphi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\prod_{x\in X}\,\omega^{\prime}(x)^{\varphi(x)}\right)\\[10.00002pt]
&\Longleftrightarrow&\displaystyle\ln\Big(({\kern 1.0pt}\varphi{\kern 1.0pt})\Big)\,+\,\sum_{x\in X}\,\varphi(x)\cdot\ln\Big(\omega(x)\Big)\leq\ln\Big(({\kern 1.0pt}\varphi{\kern 1.0pt})\Big)\,+\,\sum_{x\in X}\,\varphi(x)\cdot\ln\Big(\omega^{\prime}(x)\Big)\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\displaystyle-\sum_{x\in X}\,\frac{\varphi(x)}{\|\varphi\|}\cdot\ln\big(\omega(x)\big)\geq-\sum_{x\in X}\,\frac{\varphi(x)}{\|\varphi\|}\cdot\ln\big(\omega^{\prime}(x)\big)\\[11.99998pt]
&\Longleftrightarrow&\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\,\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)(x)\cdot\ln\Big(\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)(x)\Big)-\sum_{x\in X}\,\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)(x)\cdot\ln\big(\omega(x)\big)\\[11.99998pt]
&&\hskip 20.00003pt\displaystyle\geq\,\sum_{x\in X}\,\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)(x)\cdot\ln\Big(\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)(x)\Big)-\sum_{x\in X}\,\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)(x)\cdot\ln\big(\omega^{\prime}(x)\big)\\[8.00003pt]
&\Longleftrightarrow&\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\,\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)(x)\cdot\ln\left(\frac{\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)(x)}{\omega(x)}\right)\geq\sum_{x\in X}\,\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)(x)\cdot\ln\left(\frac{\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi)(x)}{\omega^{\prime}(x)}\right)\\[10.00002pt]
&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{KLdivEqn}}}{{\Longleftrightarrow}}}&\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}\big(\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi),\,\omega\big)\geq\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}\big(\mathsl{Flrn}(\varphi),\,\omega^{\prime}\big).\end{array}

5
Updating distributions

This section first recalls how to update a distribution with a single
predicate, basically as in (
5
). Then it introduces
two update mechanisms, for Jeffrey and for Pearl, about updating a
distribution with evidence, in the form of a multiset of predicates.

Definition 5

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
be distribution and
p\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a factor with non-zero validity
\omega\models p
. We write

\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}\in\mathcal{D}(X)
for the new, Bayesian update of

\omega
obtained as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}&\coloneqq&\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\,\frac{\omega(x)\cdot p(x)}{\omega\models p}\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}x{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle.\end{array}

(20)

Whenever we write
\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}
we implicitly assume

\omega\models p\neq 0
.

We have seen an example of how this updating works
in (
6
), by incorporating the predicate into the
distribution.

Example 1

We can now describe the physical model from
Subsection
1.1
in a mathematically precise
manner. There we started with a distribution
\omega=\frac{1}{2}|{\kern 1.00006pt}L{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{1}{3}|{\kern 1.00006pt}M{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{1}{6}|{\kern 1.00006pt}R{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle

corresponding to the division of water flows over three pipes, labeled
as left (
L
), middle (
M
) and right (
R
).

During the first update, the middle pipe was blocked. This can be
captured by the sharp predicate
\big(\mathbf{1}_{M}\big)^{\bot}=\mathbf{1}_{\{L,R\}}
corresponding to the subset
\{L,R\}\subseteq\{L,M,R\}
of open pipes. This sharp predicate has validity:

\begin{array}[]{rcccccccl}\omega\models\mathbf{1}_{\{L,R\}}&=&\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\{L,M,R\}}\omega(x)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{L,R\}}(x)&=&\omega(L)+\omega(R)&=&\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{6}&=&\frac{2}{3}.\end{array}

Updating with this predicate yields the distribution written
as
\omega^{\prime}
in Subsection
1.1
. Explicitly:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega^{\prime}\hskip 2.0pt\coloneqq\hskip 2.0pt\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{\{L,R\}}$}&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{UpdateEqn}}}{{=}}}&\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\{L,M,R\}}\frac{\omega(x)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{L,R\}}(x)}{\omega\models\mathbf{1}_{\{L,R\}}}|{\kern 1.00006pt}x{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle\\[13.99995pt]
&=&\displaystyle\frac{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot 1}{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}|{\kern 1.00006pt}L{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}\cdot 0}{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}|{\kern 1.00006pt}M{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{\nicefrac{{1}}{{6}}\cdot 1}{\nicefrac{{2}}{{3}}}|{\kern 1.00006pt}R{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\textstyle\frac{3}{4}|{\kern 1.00006pt}L{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{1}{4}|{\kern 1.00006pt}R{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle.\end{array}

The second distribution
\omega^{\prime\prime}
in
Subsection
1.1
is obtained by updating
\omega
with
the fuzzy predicate
p=\frac{2}{3}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{L}+\frac{1}{3}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{M}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{R}
. It captures
the openness of the three taps. This leads to a validity

\omega\models p=\frac{19}{36}
. It is called a normalisation factor
in Subsection
1.1
. The calculation of
\omega^{\prime\prime}

given there fits the above definition (
20
) of
the Bayesian update.

Bayesian updating satisfies several basic, standard properties that
are easy to prove.

Lemma 6

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
and
p,q\in\mathsl{Fact}(X)
be factors.

1.

\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}$}=\omega
and

\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p\mathrel{\&}q$}=\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}
; as a result, the order
of multiple updates is irrelevant;

2.

\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{x}$}=1|{\kern 1.00006pt}x{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
, for each
x\in\mathsl{supp}(\omega)
;

3.

\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle s\cdot p$}=\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}
for a scalar
s>0
;

4.

The product rule holds:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}\models q&\,=&\displaystyle\frac{\omega\models p\mathrel{\&}q}{\omega\models p}.\end{array}

5.

Bayes’ rule holds:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}\models q&\,=&\displaystyle\frac{(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models p)\cdot(\omega\models q)}{\omega\models p}.\end{array}

6.

For a sequence of factors
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\mathsl{Fact}(X)
one has:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lx@intercol\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\omega\otimes\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}$}\big)\otimes\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{2}$}\big)\otimes\cdots\otimes\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}$}\cdots\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{n-1}$}\big)\models p_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{n}$\mbox{}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\omega\models p_{1}\mathrel{\&}\cdots\mathrel{\&}p_{n}.\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\frac{1}{n!}\cdot\Big(\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\,\sum_{i}1|{\kern 1.00006pt}p_{i}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle\Big).\end{array}

The last item generalises what we have seen in
Remark
1
. Recall
from (
16
) that the Jeffrey validity of the factors
in the last item takes the form
\omega\otimes\cdots\otimes\omega\models p_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{n}=\prod_{i}\omega\models p_{i}
. It thus involves the ‘independent’ validity of each of the
factors
p_{i}
in the same distribution
\omega
. The above last
item (
6
) shows the difference with Pearl
validity, which involves ‘dependent’ validity of
p_{i+1}
in the
distribution that is updated with the previous factors
p_{1},\ldots,p_{i}
. Thus, at state
i+1
one takes into account what has already
been learned at previous stages
1,\ldots,i
.

Proof

We only prove the last item (
6
) since the
other ones are standard (see e.g.
[
24
, Lemma 5]
) and easy
to show. We use induction on
n
, where the base case for
n=1
is
obvious. Next, by Lemma
1
(
4
) and
Lemma
6
(
1
),
(
4
):

\hskip-25.00003pt\begin{array}[b]{rcl}\lx@intercol\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\omega\otimes\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}$}\big)\otimes\cdots\otimes\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}$}\cdots\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{n-1}$}\big)\otimes\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}$}\cdots\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{n}$}\big)\models p_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{n}\otimes p_{n+1}$\mbox{}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\Big(\omega\otimes\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}$}\big)\otimes\cdots\otimes\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}$}\cdots\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{n-1}$}\big)\models p_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes p_{n}\Big)\cdot\Big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}$}\cdots\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{n}$}\models\hbox to0.0pt{$p_{n+1}\Big)$\hss}\\[1.99997pt]
&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(IH)}}}{{=}}}&\Big(\omega\models p_{1}\mathrel{\&}\cdots\mathrel{\&}p_{n}\Big)\cdot\Big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}\mathrel{\&}\cdots\mathrel{\&}p_{n}$}\models p_{n+1}\Big)\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\omega\models p_{1}\mathrel{\&}\cdots\mathrel{\&}p_{n}\mathrel{\&}p_{n+1}.\end{array}

We now formulate two general results about the gains of learning.
They will be applied later for the rules of Jeffrey and Pearl. The
formulation below occurs in
[
20
]
and is proven there. For
completeness we reproduce the proof in the appendix.

Theorem 5.1

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
be a distribution.

1.

For a factor
p
on
X
with
non-zero validity
\omega\models p
one has:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}\models p&\,\geq&\omega\models p.\end{array}

2.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
be factors
on
X
, all with non-zero validity
\omega\models p_{i}
. The
uniform convex sum of updates
\omega^{\prime}\coloneqq\sum_{i}\frac{1}{n}\cdot\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{i}$}
satisfies:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\displaystyle\prod}_{i}\,(\omega^{\prime}\models p_{i})&\,\geq&{\displaystyle\prod}_{i}\,(\omega\models p_{i}).\end{array}

The first item expresses that in a Bayesian update of a distribution
with evidence / factor
p
this
p
becomes ‘more true’ in the updated
posterior distribution, than in the original prior distribution. This
is a benefit that one expects from learning. The second item is more
subtle and expresses an increase of multiplied validities.

We now give general formulations for the Jeffrey and Pearl update
mechanisms, with evidence. We have used the vertical bar
|
for
Bayesian updating in the notation
\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}
. For the Jeffrey,
and Pearl versions we make subtle variations: we change the vertical
bar
|
into

for the Jeffrey update and into

for the Pearl update, referring respectively to
the letters ‘J’ and ‘P’.

Definition 6

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
be a distribution with evidence

\psi\in\mathcal{M}\big(\mathsl{Fact}(X)\big)
.

1.

The Jeffrey update

(21)

2.

\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\psi=\mathop{\textnormal{\Large\&}}_{p\in\mathsl{supp}(\psi)}p^{\psi(p)}
, as in:

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to2.28pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

For singleton evidence

\omega{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-5.16663pt}{$\scalebox{0.9}{\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to12.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-11.75017pt\lower-1.3381pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

, so Jeffrey and Pearl
updates coincide in this trivial case with a Bayesian update. The
differences between the update modes arise for multiple pieces of
evidence.

Figure 3:
Posterior disease probabilities in the medical test scenario
from the introduction, for the two update mechanisms of Jeffrey and
Pearl, with 100 different versions of evidence
i\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathsl{pt}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle+j|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathsl{nt}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
, where
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,10\}
.

Equations (
8
) and (
9
) provide
illustrations of these two update mechanisms. Figure
3

contains plots of the updated (posterior) disease probabilities, for
Jeffrey and Pearl, using evidence of the form
i\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathsl{pt}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle+j|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathsl{nt}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
, with
i
positive and
j
negative tests, for all
values
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,10\}
. We see that in comparison with the
validities in Figure
2
there are now considerable
differences between Jeffrey and Pearl. In the Jeffrey case the plot
is rather flat, since the posterior disease probability is a convex
combination of the form:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lx@intercol\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{i}{i+j}\cdot\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsl{pt}$}(d)+\frac{j}{i+j}\cdot\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathsl{nt}$}(d),\qquad\mbox{for $1\leq i,j\leq 10$,
see~\eqref{EvicenceUpdateDefJeffreyEqn}}$\mbox{}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[3.00003pt]
&=&\frac{i}{i+j}\cdot\frac{9}{85}+\frac{j}{i+j}\cdot\frac{1}{115}\hskip 40.00006pt\mbox{see~\eqref{PostestUpdateEqn} and \eqref{NegtestUpdateEqn}.}\end{array}

In the Pearl case, on the right in Figure
3
, we see more
variation. The relevant formula is not as simple as in the Jeffrey
case. The Pearl posterior disease probability is highest when the
evidence contains a high number
i
of positive tests and a low number

j
of negative tests. This makes sense.

We proceed with some key validity increase — and other —
properties of these updates with evidence. We begin with the Jeffrey
case.

Theorem 5.2

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
be a distribution with evidence

\psi\in\mathcal{M}\big(\mathsl{Fact}(X)\big)
.

1.

Jeffrey updating increases
the Jeffrey validity:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-5.16663pt}{$\scalebox{0.9}{\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to12.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-11.75017pt\lower-1.3381pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

Item (
5
) says that you learn
nothing if you use the information from the distribution
\omega
as
evidence. More informally, you learn nothing in Jeffrey style from
what you already know. The fact that
\omega
is required to be
fractional is not essential. It is needed because we have defined
multisets of evidence to have only natural numbers as frequencies. One
can drop this limitation and allow non-negative real numbers as
frequencies. Then one can use the distribution
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
as a
multiset of point predicates itself — without multiplication with
the number
N
— and write
\omega{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-5.16663pt}{$\scalebox{0.9}{\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to12.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-11.75017pt\lower-1.3381pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 C 19.69 -0.87 18.98 -1.57 18.11 -1.57 C 17.24 -1.57 16.54 -0.87 16.54 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern 0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\omega$}=\omega
. The
same thing applies in item (
6
).

Proof

Let the evidence multinomial
\psi\in\mathcal{M}\big(\mathsl{Fact}(X)\big)
have size
K
.

1.

We write the multiset
\psi
as accumulation
\psi=\mathsl{acc}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{K})
of a sequence of factors, including multiple
occurrences, with each
p\in\mathsl{supp}(\psi)
occurring
\psi(p)
many
times in this sequence
p_{1},\ldots,p_{K}
.
Theorem
5.1
(
2
) tells us that:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega^{\prime}\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}\psi\hskip 2.0pt\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{JeffreyValidity}}}{{=}}}\hskip 2.0pt\displaystyle({\kern 1.0pt}\psi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\prod_{p\in\mathsl{supp}(\psi)}(\omega^{\prime}\models p)^{\psi(p)}&=&({\kern 1.0pt}\psi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot{\displaystyle\prod}_{i}\,(\omega^{\prime}\models p_{i})\\[-5.0pt]
&\geq&({\kern 1.0pt}\psi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot{\displaystyle\prod}_{i}\,(\omega\models p_{i})\\[5.0pt]
&=&\displaystyle({\kern 1.0pt}\psi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\prod_{p\in\mathsl{supp}(\psi)}(\omega\models p)^{\psi(p)}\hskip 2.0pt\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{JeffreyValidity}}}{{=}}}\hskip 2.0pt\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}\psi,\end{array}

where, as defined in
Theorem
5.1

5.

Suppose we can write
\omega=\sum_{i}\frac{n_{i}}{N}|{\kern 1.00006pt}x_{i}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
, with

n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
and
N=\sum_{i}n_{i}
. Then
N\cdot\omega
is the
multiset
\sum_{i}n_{i}|{\kern 1.00006pt}x_{i}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
, which we identify with the point evidence

\sum_{i}n_{i}\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathbf{1}_{x_{i}}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle
. Hence, by Lemma
6
(
2
),

\begin{array}[]{rcccccl}\omega{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-5.16663pt}{$\scalebox{0.9}{\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to12.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-11.75017pt\lower-1.3381pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 C 19.69 -0.87 18.98 -1.57 18.11 -1.57 C 17.24 -1.57 16.54 -0.87 16.54 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern 0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle N\cdot\omega$}&=&{\displaystyle\sum}_{i}\,\mathsl{Flrn}\big(N\cdot\omega\big)(x_{i})\cdot\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{x_{i}}$}&=&{\displaystyle\sum}_{i}\,\frac{n_{i}}{N}\cdot 1|{\kern 1.00006pt}x_{i}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle&=&\omega.\end{array}

We turn to Pearl’s style of updating.

5.

One learns nothing via Pearl
updates with uniform factors, that is, with scalars
s\cdot\mathbf{1}
of
the truth predicate, for
s>0
. Explicitly,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to2.28pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\psi$}&\,=&\omega\qquad\mbox{for $\;\psi={\displaystyle\sum}_{i}\,\psi(i)\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}s_{i}\cdot\mathbf{1}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle$ with $s_{i}>0$.}\end{array}

Proof

1.

Directly by Theorem
5.1
(
1
):

\begin{array}[]{rcccccl}\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to2.28pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 13.19 C 20.17 13.77 21.04 13.85 21.63 13.36 C 22.21 12.87 22.29 12 21.8 11.42 C 21.48 11.04 20.99 10.86 20.5 10.95 C 20.18 11 19.89 11.17 19.69 11.42}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\psi$}\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\psi&\,=&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\psi$}\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\psi&\,\geq&\omega\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\psi&\,=&\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\psi.\end{array}

2.

The first thing to note is that
\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\big(\psi+\chi\big)=\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)\mathrel{\&}\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\chi)
. This uses
p^{n+m}=p^{n}\mathrel{\&}p^{m}
. Then, following
Definition
3
(
2
)
one has:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\big(\psi+\chi\big)\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\displaystyle\mathop{\textnormal{\Large\&}}_{p\in\mathsl{Fact}(X)}\,p^{(\psi+\chi)(p)}&=&\displaystyle\mathop{\textnormal{\Large\&}}_{p\in\mathsl{Fact}(X)}\,p^{\psi(p)+\chi(p)}\\
&=&\displaystyle\mathop{\textnormal{\Large\&}}_{p\in\mathsl{Fact}(X)}\,p^{\psi(p)}\mathrel{\&}p^{\chi(p)}\\
&=&\displaystyle\mathop{\textnormal{\Large\&}}_{p\in\mathsl{Fact}(X)}\,p^{\psi(p)}\mathrel{\&}\displaystyle\mathop{\textnormal{\Large\&}}_{p\in\mathsl{Fact}(X)}\,p^{\chi(p)}\\
&=&\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)\mathrel{\&}\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\chi).\end{array}

Now, using Proposition
6
(
4
):

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to2.28pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 13.19 C 20.17 13.77 21.04 13.85 21.63 13.36 C 22.21 12.87 22.29 12 21.8 11.42 C 21.48 11.04 20.99 10.86 20.5 10.95 C 20.18 11 19.89 11.17 19.69 11.42}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\psi$}\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\chi\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)$}\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\chi)&=&\displaystyle\frac{\omega\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)\mathrel{\&}\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\chi)}{\omega\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)}\\[8.00003pt]
&=&\displaystyle\frac{\omega\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi+\chi)}{\omega\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)}\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\displaystyle\frac{\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\psi+\chi}{\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\psi}.\end{array}

Similarly, using
Proposition
6
(
5
),
one obtains:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to2.28pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\psi$}\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\chi\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)$}\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\chi)&=&\displaystyle\frac{\omega\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)\mathrel{\&}\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\chi)}{\omega\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)}\\[8.00003pt]
&=&\displaystyle\frac{(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\chi)$}\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)\cdot(\omega\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\chi))}{\omega\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)}\\[8.00003pt]
&=&\displaystyle\frac{(\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to2.28pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

Let the evidence be of the form
\psi=\sum_{i}\,\psi(i)\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}s_{i}\cdot\mathbf{1}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle
with
s_{i}>0
. Then
\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\psi=\mathop{\mathrel{\&}_{i}}(s_{i}\cdot\mathbf{1})^{\psi(i)}=s\cdot\mathbf{1}
, where
s=\prod_{i}s_{i}^{\psi(i)}>0
. Then, by
Proposition
6
(
3
),
(
1
), in:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern 0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\psi$}&=&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\psi$}&=&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle s\cdot\mathbf{1}$}&=&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}$}&=&\omega.\end{array}

We thus see that X-updating increases X-validity for
X\in\big\{\mbox{Jeffrey},\mbox{Pearl}\big\}
, see
Theorem
5.2
(
1
)
and
Theorem
5.3
(
1
).
Such learning makes us appropriately wiser. At the end of
Section
2
we have seen that mixing validity and
update of different kinds may lead to a decrease of validity —
making us less wise. We can now formulate this more precisely: for the
prior disease distribution
\omega=\frac{1}{20}|{\kern 1.00006pt}d{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{19}{20}|{\kern 1.00006pt}d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle

\psi=2|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathsl{pt}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+1|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathsl{nt}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
one has validity decreases after
(the wrong kind of) updates:

A final question that we consider in this section is: what happens if
one tries to update a distribution
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
with
inconsistent evidence? We take this inconsistent evidence to be of the
form
\psi=1\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathbf{1}_{U}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle+1\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathbf{1}_{\neg U}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle
, with two
sharp predicates
\mathbf{1}_{U}
and
\mathbf{1}_{\neg U}
, for a non-empty
subset
U\subseteq\mathsl{supp}(\omega)
with non-empty complement
\neg U=\mathsl{supp}(\omega)\setminus U
. The Jeffrey update with such inconsistent
evidence yields a convex combination of the two updates:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-5.16663pt}{$\scalebox{0.9}{\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to12.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-11.75017pt\lower-1.3381pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 C 19.69 -0.87 18.98 -1.57 18.11 -1.57 C 17.24 -1.57 16.54 -0.87 16.54 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern 0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\psi$}&=&\frac{1}{2}\cdot\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{U}$}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{\neg U}$}.\end{array}

(23)

This can be interpreted as a ‘superposition’ of the two
updated distributions. To be explicit, the
update
\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{U}$}
is the normalised restriction of

\omega
to the subset
U
, as in:

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{U}$}&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{UpdateEqn}}}{{=}}}&\displaystyle\sum_{x\in U}\frac{\omega(x)}{\omega\models\mathbf{1}_{U}}\,\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}x{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle&=&\displaystyle\sum_{x\in U}\frac{\omega(x)}{\sum_{y\in U}\omega(y)}\,\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}x{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle.\end{array}

Thus, the two distributions
\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{U}$}
and

\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{\neg U}$}
have disjoint supports, but happily
live together in the Jeffrey update (
23
).
This reinforces the idea that Jeffrey’s approach involves a convex
combination of independent updates.

The Pearl update would be of the form
\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{U}\mathrel{\&}\mathbf{1}_{\neg U}$}=\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{U}$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{1}_{\neg U}$}
. We notice that the
conjunction
\mathrel{\&}\psi=\mathbf{1}_{U}\mathrel{\&}\mathbf{1}_{\neg U}=\mathbf{1}_{U\cap\neg U}=\mathbf{1}_{\emptyset}=\mathbf{0}
yields
falsity. Updating with the predicate
\mathbf{0}
is impossible, since its
validity is
0
.

6
Channels

At this stage we have seen the basics of validity and updating
according to Jeffrey and Pearl, and how these updates make us
appropriately wiser. In the set up so far we have used a distribution

\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
on a set
X
, and evidence

\psi\in\mathcal{M}\big(\mathsl{Fact}(X)\big)
on this same set
X
. We now
generalise this situation to allow evidence on a different set, say

Y
. This is a common situation in which information on
Y
is
observable, and information on
X
is hidden, often called latent. The
connection between the sets
X
and
Y
happens via what is called a
channel, forming a generative model. This section outlines the role
that these channels play in probabilistic updating.

Channels formalise the idea of conditional probabilities. They carry a
rich mathematical structure that can be used in compositional
reasoning, with both sequential and parallel composition and with
reversal. The concept of a channel has emerged in various forms,
namely as conditional probability, stochastic matrix, probabilistic
classifier, Markov kernel, statistical model, conditional probability
table (in Bayesian network), probabilistic function / computation, and
finally as Kleisli map (in category theory).

We begin with the definition of a channel and with the associated
forward and backward transformation mechanisms.

Definition 7

Let
X,Y
be arbitrary sets.

1.

A channel
c
from
X
to
Y
is a function of the form
c\colon X\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(Y)
. It assigns a distribution
c(x)\in\mathcal{D}(Y)
to an arbitrary element
x\in X
, and thus corresponds to a
conditional probability
\mathsl{P}(y|x)
. We write such a channel as

c\colon X\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\to$\cr\hfil\!$\hbox{\hbox to2.2pt{\vbox to2.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.09999pt\lower-1.09999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\ignorespaces}}
{{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.97 0 C 0.97 0.53 0.53 0.97 0 0.97 C -0.53 0.97 -0.97 0.53 -0.97 0 C -0.97 -0.53 -0.53 -0.97 0 -0.97 C 0.53 -0.97 0.97 -0.53 0.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.2pt} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\hfil\cr}}}Y
, with a circle on the shaft of the arrow.

2.

For a channel
c\colon X\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\to$\cr\hfil\!$\hbox{\hbox to2.2pt{\vbox to2.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.09999pt\lower-1.09999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\ignorespaces}}
{{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.97 0 C 0.97 0.53 0.53 0.97 0 0.97 C -0.53 0.97 -0.97 0.53 -0.97 0 C -0.97 -0.53 -0.53 -0.97 0 -0.97 C 0.53 -0.97 0.97 -0.53 0.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.2pt} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\hfil\cr}}}Y
and a distribution

\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
we can form the pushforward distribution
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega
on
Y
, as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega&\coloneqq&\displaystyle\sum_{y\in Y}\left(\sum_{x\in X}\,\omega(x)\cdot c(x)(y)\right)\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}y{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle\,\in\,\mathcal{D}(Y).\end{array}

(24)

This pushforward
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega
is also called the
prediction, see Example
2
below.

3.

For a channel
c\colon X\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\to$\cr\hfil\!$\hbox{\hbox to2.2pt{\vbox to2.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.09999pt\lower-1.09999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\ignorespaces}}
{{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.97 0 C 0.97 0.53 0.53 0.97 0 0.97 C -0.53 0.97 -0.97 0.53 -0.97 0 C -0.97 -0.53 -0.53 -0.97 0 -0.97 C 0.53 -0.97 0.97 -0.53 0.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.2pt} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\hfil\cr}}}Y
and an observation
q\colon Y\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
we can form the pullback observation
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}q
on

X
, via:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\big(c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}q\big)(x)&\coloneqq&\displaystyle\sum_{y\in Y}c(x)(y)\cdot q(y).\end{array}

(25)

This pullback operation
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}(-)
restricts to factors
and to fuzzy predicates, but not to sharp predicates.

Using point predicates we can equivalently write (when the
set
X
is finite):

\begin{array}[]{rcl}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}q&\coloneqq&\displaystyle\sum_{x\in X}\left(\sum_{y\in Y}c(x)(y)\cdot q(y)\right)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{x}.\end{array}

(26)

The following easy result shows how forward and backward transformation
are closely related via validity. The proof follows from unravelling
the relevant definitions.

Lemma 7

For a channel
c\colon X\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\to$\cr\hfil\!$\hbox{\hbox to2.2pt{\vbox to2.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.09999pt\lower-1.09999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\ignorespaces}}
{{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.97 0 C 0.97 0.53 0.53 0.97 0 0.97 C -0.53 0.97 -0.97 0.53 -0.97 0 C -0.97 -0.53 -0.53 -0.97 0 -0.97 C 0.53 -0.97 0.97 -0.53 0.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.2pt} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\hfil\cr}}}Y
with a distribution

\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
on its domain
X
and an observation
q\in\mathsl{Obs}(Y)

on its codomain one has the following equality of validities.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\big(c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega\big)\models q&\,=&\omega\models\big(c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}q).\end{array}

(27)

Hidden in the medical test description in the introduction there is a
channel. We make it explicit below. In this situation the
distributions have only two elements in their support, so the
situation may look rather trivial. But channels may involve much more
complicated distributions, where the approach of the above definition
provides a clear general methodology.

Example 2

We will redescribe the medical test from the introduction as a channel

c\colon D\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\to$\cr\hfil\!$\hbox{\hbox to2.2pt{\vbox to2.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.09999pt\lower-1.09999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\ignorespaces}}
{{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.97 0 C 0.97 0.53 0.53 0.97 0 0.97 C -0.53 0.97 -0.97 0.53 -0.97 0 C -0.97 -0.53 -0.53 -0.97 0 -0.97 C 0.53 -0.97 0.97 -0.53 0.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.2pt} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\hfil\cr}}}T
, where
D=\{d,d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}\}
is the disease set and

T=\{p,n\}
is the set of test outcomes. This test channel
c
is
determined by the sensitivity of
\frac{9}{10}
and specificity of

\frac{3}{5}
, namely as:

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}c(d)&=&\frac{9}{10}|{\kern 1.00006pt}p{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{1}{10}|{\kern 1.00006pt}n{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle&\qquad\mbox{and}&c(d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot})&=&\frac{2}{5}|{\kern 1.00006pt}p{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{3}{5}|{\kern 1.00006pt}n{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle.\end{array}

One sees that in the first case, in presence of the disease

d
, the channel outcome
c(d)
gives a
\frac{9}{10}
chance of a
positive test. Similarly, in absence of the disease
d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}
, the
distribution
c(d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot})
gives a
\frac{3}{5}
chance of a negative
test.

We assumed a prevalence of
5\%
, corresponding to the prior disease
distribution
\omega=\frac{1}{20}|{\kern 1.00006pt}d{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{19}{20}|{\kern 1.00006pt}d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
. The predicted test outcome distribution is
the pushforward
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega
of the prior along the channel. It
is computed as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ChannelForwardEqn}}}{{=}}}&\displaystyle\left(\sum_{x\in D}\omega(x)\cdot c(x)(p)\right)|{\kern 1.00006pt}p{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\left(\sum_{x\in D}\omega(x)\cdot c(x)(n)\right)|{\kern 1.00006pt}n{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle\\[11.99998pt]
&=&\Big(\frac{1}{20}\cdot\frac{9}{10}+\frac{19}{20}\cdot\frac{2}{5}\Big)|{\kern 1.00006pt}p{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\Big(\frac{1}{20}\cdot\frac{1}{10}+\frac{19}{20}\cdot\frac{3}{5}\Big)|{\kern 1.00006pt}n{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle\\[6.00006pt]
&=&\frac{17}{40}|{\kern 1.00006pt}p{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{23}{40}|{\kern 1.00006pt}n{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle.\end{array}

Backward transformation of (point) predicates is also relevant in this
example, since in this way we rediscover the positive / negative test
predicates
\mathsl{pt}
,
\mathsl{nt}
on
D
that we used before:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}\mathbf{1}_{p}&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ChannelBackwardPointEqn}}}{{=}}}&\displaystyle\left(\sum_{y\in T}c(d)(y)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{p}(y)\right)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d}+\left(\sum_{y\in T}c(d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot})(y)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{p}(y)\right)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}}\\[13.99995pt]
&=&c(d)(p)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d}+c(d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot})(p)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}}\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\frac{9}{10}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d}+\frac{2}{5}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}}\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\mathsl{pt}\\[3.99994pt]
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}\mathbf{1}_{n}&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ChannelBackwardPointEqn}}}{{=}}}&\displaystyle\left(\sum_{y\in T}c(d)(y)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}(y)\right)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d}+\left(\sum_{y\in T}c(d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot})(y)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{n}(y)\right)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}}\\[13.99995pt]
&=&c(d)(n)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d}+c(d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot})(n)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}}\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\frac{1}{10}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d}+\frac{3}{5}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}}\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\mathsl{nt}.\end{array}

In order to be able to speak of validity and updating along a channel
we transform evidence along this channel. Then we can easily define
channel-based validity and updating.

Definition 8

Let
c\colon X\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\to$\cr\hfil\!$\hbox{\hbox to2.2pt{\vbox to2.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.09999pt\lower-1.09999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\ignorespaces}}
{{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.97 0 C 0.97 0.53 0.53 0.97 0 0.97 C -0.53 0.97 -0.97 0.53 -0.97 0 C -0.97 -0.53 -0.53 -0.97 0 -0.97 C 0.53 -0.97 0.97 -0.53 0.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.2pt} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\hfil\cr}}}Y
be a channel, with a distribution

\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
on its domain
X
and with evidence

\psi\in\mathcal{M}\big(\mathsl{Fact}(Y)\big)
on its codomain
Y
.

1.

We write
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\ggg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\ggg=$}}}\psi
for the evidence on
X
obtained by transforming factor-wise
along
c
. Thus:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\ggg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\ggg=$}}}\psi&\coloneqq&\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathsl{Fact}(Y)}\,\psi(q)\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}q{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle\,\in\,\mathcal{M}\big(\mathsl{Fact}(X)\big).\end{array}

This can also be described via the functoriality of

\mathcal{M}
, applied to the function
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}(-)\colon\mathsl{Fact}(Y)\rightarrow\mathsl{Fact}(X)
.

2.

The Jeffrey validity of

\psi
in
\omega
along the channel
c
is now defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\ggg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\ggg=$}}}\psi&\,=&\displaystyle({\kern 1.0pt}\psi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\prod_{q\in\mathsl{Fact}(Y)}\,\big(\omega\models c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}q\big)^{\psi(q)}\\[10.00002pt]
&\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{TransformationValidityEqn}}}{{=}}}&\displaystyle({\kern 1.0pt}\psi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\prod_{q\in\mathsl{Fact}(Y)}\,\big(c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega\models q\big)^{\psi(q)}\\[10.00002pt]
&=&c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}\psi.\end{array}

(28)

By construction, this Jeffrey validity satisfies an analogue
of Lemma
7
.

Similarly, the Pearl validity along the channel is defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\ggg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\ggg=$}}}\psi&\,=&\displaystyle({\kern 1.0pt}\psi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\Big(\omega\models\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\big(c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\ggg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\ggg=$}}}\psi\big)\Big)\\[5.0pt]
&=&\displaystyle({\kern 1.0pt}\psi{\kern 1.0pt})\cdot\Big(\omega\models\mathop{\textnormal{\Large\&}}_{q\in\mathsl{Fact}(Y)}\,(c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}q)^{\psi(q)}\Big).\end{array}

(29)

(This is
not
equal to
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\psi

since transformation
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}(-)\colon\mathsl{Fact}(Y)\rightarrow\mathsl{Fact}(X)
does not preserve conjunctions
\mathrel{\&}
.)

3.

Along the same lines, the
Jeffrey update along a channel with evidence
\psi
is:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-5.16663pt}{$\scalebox{0.9}{\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to12.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-11.75017pt\lower-1.3381pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 C 19.69 -0.87 18.98 -1.57 18.11 -1.57 C 17.24 -1.57 16.54 -0.87 16.54 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern 0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-4.52083pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-4.52083pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 1.50694pt}\scriptstyle\ggg{\kern-0.42192pt}={\kern 1.05486pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\ggg=$}}}\psi$}&=&\displaystyle\sum_{q\in\mathsl{Fact}{Y}}\,\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi)(q)\cdot\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-4.52083pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 1.50694pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.42192pt}={\kern 1.05486pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}q$}.\end{array}

And the Pearl update along a channel is:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to2.28pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 13.19 C 20.17 13.77 21.04 13.85 21.63 13.36 C 22.21 12.87 22.29 12 21.8 11.42 C 21.48 11.04 20.99 10.86 20.5 10.95 C 20.18 11 19.89 11.17 19.69 11.42}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-4.52083pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-4.52083pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 1.50694pt}\scriptstyle\ggg{\kern-0.42192pt}={\kern 1.05486pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\ggg=$}}}\psi$}&=&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathrel{\&}_{q}(c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-4.52083pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 1.50694pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.42192pt}={\kern 1.05486pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}q)^{\psi(q)}$}.\end{array}

We can now check that the Jeffrey and Pearl updates
in (
8
) and (

) are updates
along the test channel
c\colon D\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\to$\cr\hfil\!$\hbox{\hbox to2.2pt{\vbox to2.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.09999pt\lower-1.09999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\ignorespaces}}

from
Example
2
, using the point evidence

2|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathbf{1}_{p}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+1|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathbf{1}_{n}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
.

The special case of updating with point evidence along a channel is
worth a closer look. The reformulations in terms of multinomials come
from
[
23
]
. The formulation of Jeffrey’s update rule via a
dagger channel occurs in
[
18
]
and the associated decrease
of KL-divergence in
item (
3
) was first
identified in
[
19
]
. In
[
23
]
one can also find
a description of Jeffrey updating along a channel via variational
inference.

Proposition 1

Let
c\colon X\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\to$\cr\hfil\!$\hbox{\hbox to2.2pt{\vbox to2.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.09999pt\lower-1.09999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\ignorespaces}}
{{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.97 0 C 0.97 0.53 0.53 0.97 0 0.97 C -0.53 0.97 -0.97 0.53 -0.97 0 C -0.97 -0.53 -0.53 -0.97 0 -0.97 C 0.53 -0.97 0.97 -0.53 0.97 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}{\ignorespaces}{}{\ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.2pt} \ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.38 0 C 1.38 0.76 0.76 1.38 0 1.38 C -0.76 1.38 -1.38 0.76 -1.38 0 C -1.38 -0.76 -0.76 -1.38 0 -1.38 C 0.76 -1.38 1.38 -0.76 1.38 0 Z M 0 0}{} \ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}{\ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\hfil\cr}}}Y
be a channel, with a distribution

\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
and with point evidence
\psi\in\mathcal{M}[K](Y)
, so that
c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\ggg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\ggg=$}}}\psi
is
\sum_{y}\psi(y)\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}\mathbf{1}_{y}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle
. Then:

1.

The
Jeffrey validity of
\psi
along
c
can be expressed as multinomial
probability:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\ggg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\ggg=$}}}\psi&\,=&\mathsl{mn}[K]\big(c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega\big)(\psi).\end{array}

2.

The Pearl
validity of
\psi
along
c
is:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}c\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\ggg{\kern-6.45831pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle\ggg{\kern-0.60275pt}={\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\ggg=$}}}\psi&\,=&\big(\mathsl{mn}[K](c)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega\big)(\psi),\end{array}

where
\mathsl{mn}[K](c)\coloneqq\mathsl{mn}[K]\mathrel{\circ}c\colon X\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(Y)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\big(\mathcal{M}[K](Y)\big)
.

3.

The Jeffrey
update of
\omega
with point evidence
\psi
along
c
can be
described as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\omega{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-5.16663pt}{$\scalebox{0.9}{\hbox to2.68pt{\vbox to12.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-11.75017pt\lower-1.3381pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

where
c^{{\dagger}}_{\omega}\colon Y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\to$\cr\hfil\!$\hbox{\hbox to2.2pt{\vbox to2.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.09999pt\lower-1.09999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\ignorespaces}}
