{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 13.78 L 23.62 13.78}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 11.81 L 22.64 11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern-1.07639pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle i|{\kern 0.81949pt}t\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-4.52083pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 1.50694pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.42192pt}={\kern 1.05486pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}\mathbf{1}_{p}{\kern 0.81949pt}\rangle+j|{\kern 0.81949pt}t\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\gg{\kern-4.52083pt}=$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 1.50694pt}\scriptstyle\gg{\kern-0.42192pt}={\kern 1.05486pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\gg=$}}}\mathbf{1}_{n}{\kern 0.81949pt}\rangle$}&=&\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to3.25pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 13.78 L 23.62 13.78}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 11.81 L 22.64 11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern-1.07639pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle i|{\kern 0.81949pt}\mathsl{pt}{\kern 0.81949pt}\rangle+j|{\kern 0.81949pt}\mathsl{nt}{\kern 0.81949pt}\rangle$}\;\in\;\mathcal{D}(D).\end{array}

The the VFE posterior disease probabilities

\omega_{i,j}(d)
give the following plot — like in
Figure
3
for Jeffrey and Pearl.

This plot looks much like the one for Jeffrey in
Figure
3
. That is not strange since VFE updating
approximates Jeffrey updating, as we have just seen.

We then check for the expected decrease of KL-divergence via
VFE update in:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}\big(\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi_{i,j}),\,t\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega_{i,j}\big)&\,\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\leq}}}&\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}\big(\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi_{i,j}),\,t\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega\big).\end{array}

This fails for 37 out of 100 possible cases, for

i,j\in\{1,\ldots,10\}
. For instance, already for
i=j=1
we have a
uniform state of the world
\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi_{1,1})=\frac{1}{2}|{\kern 1.00006pt}p{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}|{\kern 1.00006pt}n{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle
with a positive and negative test outcome that we
wish to get close to via learning. Stepwise, this works as follows.

\begin{array}[]{lcrcl}\mbox{prior}&&\omega&=&0.05|{\kern 1.00006pt}d{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+0.95|{\kern 1.00006pt}d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle\\
\mbox{prior prediction}&&t\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega&=&0.425|{\kern 1.00006pt}p{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+0.575|{\kern 1.00006pt}n{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle\\
\mbox{VFE posterior}&&\omega_{1,1}&=&0.031|{\kern 1.00006pt}d{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+0.969|{\kern 1.00006pt}d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle\\
\mbox{posterior prediction}&&t\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega_{1,1}&=&0.416|{\kern 1.00006pt}p{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+0.584|{\kern 1.00006pt}n{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle\\
\mbox{prior divergence}&&\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}\big(\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi_{1,1}),\,t\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega\big)&=&0.0164\\
\mbox{posterior divergence}&&\mathsl{D}_{\mathsl{KL}}\big(\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi_{1,1}),\,t\mathrel{\mathchoice{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$=\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$={\kern-6.45831pt}\ll$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{${\kern 2.15277pt}\scriptstyle={\kern-0.60275pt}\ll{\kern 1.50694pt}$}}{\scalebox{-0.5}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle=\ll$}}}\omega_{1,1}\big)&=&0.0208.\end{array}

Thus, VFE updating takes us further away from the
goal distribution
\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi_{1,1})
.

This is puzzling: the stated aim in predictive coding is error
correction in the form of KL-divergence reduction. In our medical
example this reduction fails for a bit more than one third of the
cases. In contrast, the decrease of KL-divergence with Jeffrey
updating holds in all cases, see
Theorem
5.2
(
2
).
It seems that predictive coding should not use VFE updating, but
use Jeffrey updating instead, at least if it really aims to achieve
error correction as goal of updating.

In the end we ask ourselves in what sense does VFE updating make us
wiser? Does it increase Jeffrey and/or Pearl validity? It turns out
that it does not increase Jeffrey validity, in general. VFE updating
does increase Pearl validity, but not as much as Pearl updating.

Theorem 7.1

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
be distribution with evidence

\psi\in\mathcal{M}\big(\mathsl{Fact}(X)\big)
.

1.

VFE updating does not increase
Jeffrey validity, in general.

2.

VFE updating does increase
Pearl validity, but not as much as Pearl updating itself:

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\psi&\,\leq&\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to3.25pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 13.78 L 23.62 13.78}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 11.81 L 22.64 11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern-1.07639pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\psi$}\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\psi&\,\leq&\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to2.28pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 13.19 C 20.17 13.77 21.04 13.85 21.63 13.36 C 22.21 12.87 22.29 12 21.8 11.42 C 21.48 11.04 20.99 10.86 20.5 10.95 C 20.18 11 19.89 11.17 19.69 11.42}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern-0.86108pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\psi$}\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny P}}}}\,}\psi\end{array}

(35)

Proof

1.

We can reformulate the counterexample from
Remark
4
. For evidence
\chi=1\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathsl{pt}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle+1\big|{\kern 1.00006pt}\mathsl{nt}{\kern 1.00006pt}\big\rangle
with one positive and one
negative test, and prior
\omega=0.05|{\kern 1.00006pt}d{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle+0.95|{\kern 1.00006pt}d^{\scriptscriptstyle\bot}{\kern 1.00006pt}\rangle

we get:

\begin{array}[]{rcccccl}\omega\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}{\chi}&\approx&0.489&>&0.486&\approx&\omega{\kern 1.29167pt}\raisebox{-6.02773pt}{$\scalebox{1.0}{\hbox to3.25pt{\vbox to11.07pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-14.02638pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 L 19.69 14.76}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 13.78 L 23.62 13.78}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 11.81 L 22.64 11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$}{\kern-1.07639pt}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}\mathbin{\smash{\models_{\hskip-3.61664pt{\raisebox{-1.20552pt}{\tiny J}}}}\,}{\chi}.\end{array}

2.

We start with a number of auxiliary results (for
K\geq 1
).

(i)

\big(\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi)\big)^{K}=\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\psi
, where
K=\|\psi\|
;

(ii)

\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q\,\leq\,\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K}$}\models q
;

(iii)

\omega\models q^{K}\,\leq\,\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q^{K}
;

(iv)

\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q^{K}\,\leq\,\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K}$}\models q^{K}
.

The first inequality in (
35
) is then
an instance of point (
2iii
), for
q=\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi)
. The second inequality in (
35
) is
an instance (
2iv
).

It thus suffices to prove these points (
2i
) –
(
2iv
). For (
2i
) we use:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\big(\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi)\big)^{K}(x)\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\big(\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi)\big)(x)^{K}&=&\displaystyle\left(\prod_{p\in\mathsl{supp}(\psi)}\,p(x)^{\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi)(p)}\right)^{K}\\[13.99995pt]
&=&\displaystyle\prod_{p\in\mathsl{supp}(\psi)}\,p(x)^{\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi)(p)\cdot K}\\[11.99998pt]
&=&\displaystyle\prod_{p\in\mathsl{supp}(\psi)}\,p(x)^{\psi(p)}\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\big(\mathrel{\&}\psi\big)(x).\end{array}

The equation in (
2ii
) obviously holds for
K=1
. The
induction step works as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K+1}$}\models q\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K}\,\mathrel{\&}\,q$}\models q&=&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K}$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q&\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{PredicateUpdateLem}~\eqref{PredicateUpdateLemConj}}\\[1.99997pt]
&\geq&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K}$}\models q&\mbox{by Theorem~\ref{UpdateGainThm}~\eqref{UpdateGainThmSingle}}\\[1.99997pt]
&\geq&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q&\mbox{by induction hypothesis.}\end{array}

The case
K=1
of (
2iii
) is covered by
Theorem
5.1
(
1
). For the
induction step we use:

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\lx@intercol\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\omega|_{q}\models q^{K+1}$\mbox{}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\omega|_{q}\models q\mathrel{\&}q^{K}\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\big(\omega|_{q}\models q\big)\cdot\big(\omega|_{q}|_{q}\models q^{K}\big)&\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{PredicateUpdateLem}~\eqref{PredicateUpdateLemProd}}\\[1.99997pt]
&\geq&\big(\omega\models q\big)\cdot\big(\omega|_{q}\models q^{K}\big)&\mbox{by Theorem~\ref{UpdateGainThm}~\eqref{UpdateGainThmSingle} and
induction hypothesis}\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\omega\models q^{K+1}&\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{PredicateUpdateLem}~\eqref{PredicateUpdateLemProd} again.}\end{array}

The case
K=1
trivially holds for (
2iv
). Next:

\begin{array}[b]{rcll}\lx@intercol\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K+1}$}\models q^{K+1}$\mbox{}\hfil\lx@intercol\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K+1}$}\models q\mathrel{\&}q^{K}\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K+1}$}\models q\big)\cdot\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K+1}$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q^{K}\big)&\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{PredicateUpdateLem}~\eqref{PredicateUpdateLemProd}}\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K}$}\models q\big)\cdot\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q^{K}$}\models q^{K}\big)&\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{PredicateUpdateLem}~\eqref{PredicateUpdateLemConj}}\\[1.99997pt]
&\geq&\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q\big)\cdot\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q^{K}\big)&\mbox{by~\eqref{VFEUpdateThmB} and induction hypothesis}\\[1.99997pt]
&\geq&\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q\big)\cdot\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q^{K}\big)&\mbox{by Theorem~\ref{UpdateGainThm}~\eqref{UpdateGainThmSingle}
and~\eqref{VFEUpdateThmC}}\\[1.99997pt]
&=&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}\models q^{K+1}&\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{PredicateUpdateLem}~\eqref{PredicateUpdateLemProd} again.}\end{array}

In predictive coding one chooses to approximate distributions for
efficiency reasons — especially in the context of continuous
probability. The problem lies in the computation of the normalisation
constants. In Jeffrey’s update
rule (
21
) one has to compute a
normalisation constant
\omega\models p
for each factor

p\in\mathsl{supp}(\psi)
in the evidence. In the Pearl and VFE update
rules (
22
), (
31
)
only one normalisation constant needs to be computed.

In practice, the Pearl and VFE approaches have the problem that the
conjunctions
\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\psi)=\mathop{\mathrel{\&}_{p}}p^{\psi(p)}
and

\mathop{\mathrel{\&}}(\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi))=\mathop{\mathrel{\&}_{p}}p^{\mathsl{Flrn}(\psi)(p)}
may
lead to many multiplications of small numbers, giving outcomes that
become too small to be reliable. One can do the updates successively,
using the equation
\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p\mathrel{\&}q$}=\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle q$}
from
Lemma
6
(
1
), but
then one has to do multiple normalisations. In practice, Pearl’s
updates are most conveniently done in a conjugate prior situation, so
that one can directly update the parameters, without computing any
distributions.

We may cautiously conclude that the mathematical clarifications about
validity and updating in this paper have not (yet) fully clarified
which update mechanism should be used in predictive coding and that a
more detailed explanation and analysis is needed.

8
Concluding remarks

The topic of this paper is updating of probability distributions in
the face of multiple pieces of evidence. As shown, there are several
possible approaches, associated with Jeffrey, Pearl and VFE, with
different properties. Guarantees, like validity increase or divergence
decrease, only work for the right combination of validity and
updating. Hence, awareness of the different approaches is relevant in
practical situations. As the small survey with the medical tests
shows, such awareness is not common, not even among academic
professionals working in this area.

This article synthesises an earlier line of work of the author on
probabilistic
updating
[
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
,
23
]
,
concentrating on the approaches of Jeffrey and Pearl. These two
approaches occur in the literature, but are not always identified as
such. For instance, in
[
23
]
it is shown that Jeffrey’s
update rule is at the heart of the Expectation Maximisation and Latent
Dirichlet Allocation algorithms.

In contrast to the setting of this earlier work, the current paper
puts the emphasis on multiple pieces of evidence as the key
distinguishing element between Jeffrey and Pearl. At first, notably
in
[
18
,
19
]
, the difference between Jeffrey and
Pearl involved treating evidence as distribution or as predicate. The
current approach combines these perspectives by seeing evidence as a
multiset of predicates (or factors), which can be turned into a
distribution via frequentist learning
\mathsl{Flrn}
. The difference between
the validity / update rules of Jeffrey and Pearl lies in the
assumption of independence (or not). Also, in earlier work the
difference between Jeffrey and Pearl was described in the setting with
a generative model, given by both a channel and a distribution on its
domain. Here, in contrast, the different forms of updating are defined
in a simpler setting with only a prior distribution and with a
multiset of evidence. The more complex generative situation with a
channel is treated here as a special case.

A point on the horizon is a symbolic logic for probabilistic reasoning
that incorporates the various forms of probabilistic updating, with
appropriate derivation rules.

Appendix 0.A
Appendix, with the proof of Theorem
5.1

We first describe an auxiliary result which we shall call the
sum-increase lemma. It is a special (discrete) case of a more general
result
[
2
, Thm. 2.1]
. It describes how to find increases
for sum expressions in general.

Lemma 9

Let
X,Y
be finite sets, with a function
F\colon X\times Y\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. For each element
x\in X
, write
F_{1}(x)\coloneqq\sum_{y\in Y}F(x,y)
for the sum that we wish to
increase. Assume that there is an
x^{\prime}\in X
with:

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}x^{\prime}&\in&\underset{z}{\argmax}\;G(x,z)\qquad\mbox{where}\qquad G(x,z)&\coloneqq&\displaystyle\sum_{y\in Y}\,F(x,y)\cdot\ln\big(F(z,y)\big).\end{array}

Then
F_{1}(x^{\prime})\geq F_{1}(x)
.

Proof

Let
x^{\prime}
be an element where
G(x,-)\colon Y\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
takes
its maximum. This
x^{\prime}
satisfies
F_{1}(x^{\prime})\geq F_{1}(x)
, since:

\begin{array}[b]{rcl}\displaystyle\ln\left(\frac{F_{1}(x^{\prime})}{F_{1}(x)}\right)&=&\displaystyle\ln\left(\displaystyle\sum_{y\in Y}\,\frac{F(x^{\prime},y)}{F_{1}(x)}\right)\\[13.99995pt]
&=&\displaystyle\ln\left(\displaystyle\sum_{y\in Y}\,\frac{F(x,y)}{F_{1}(x)}\cdot\frac{F(x^{\prime},y)}{F(x,y)}\right)\\[13.99995pt]
&\geq&\displaystyle\sum_{y\in Y}\,\frac{F(x,y)}{F_{1}(x)}\cdot\ln\left(\frac{F(x^{\prime},y)}{F(x,y)}\right)\qquad\mbox{by Jensen's inequality}\\[11.99998pt]
&=&\displaystyle\frac{1}{F_{1}(x)}\cdot\sum_{y\in Y}\,F(x,y)\cdot\Big(\ln\big(F(x^{\prime},y)\big)-\ln\big(F(x,y)\big)\Big)\\[8.99994pt]
&=&\displaystyle\frac{1}{F_{1}(x)}\cdot\Big(G(x,x^{\prime})-G(x,x)\Big)\hskip 2.0pt\geq\hskip 2.0pt0.\end{array}

We use this result to prove the two items of Theorem
5.1

separately.

Proof
(of Theorem
5.1
(
1
))

We start from a validity
\omega\models p
, where
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)

and
p\in\mathsl{Fact}(X)
, which we would like to increase, by changing the
distribution
\omega
, while keeping
p
fixed. We apply
Lemma
9
with
F\colon\mathcal{D}(X)\times X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
given by
F(\omega,x)=\omega(x)\cdot p(x)
. Then

F_{1}(\omega)=\sum_{x}F(\omega,x)=\sum_{x}\omega(x)\cdot p(x)=\omega\models p
.

We have
G(\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\sum_{x}\omega(x)\cdot p(x)\cdot\ln\big(\omega^{\prime}(x)\cdot p(x)\big)
and we wish to find a maximum of

G(\omega,-)\colon\mathcal{D}(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Let
\mathsl{supp}(\omega)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
; we will use variables
v_{i}
for the values

\omega^{\prime}(x_{i})
that we wish to find, by seeing
G(\omega,-)
as a
function
\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with constraints.

These constraints are handled via the Lagrange multiplier method for
finding the maximum (see
e.g.

[
3
, §2.2]
). We
keep
\omega
fixed and consider a new function
H
, also known as the
Lagrangian, with an additional parameter
\kappa
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H(\vec{v},\kappa)&\coloneqq&G(\omega,\vec{v})-\kappa\cdot\big((\sum_{i}v_{i})-1\big)\\
&=&\sum_{i}\omega(x_{i})\cdot p(x_{i})\cdot\ln\big(v_{i}\cdot p(x_{i})\big)-\kappa\cdot\big((\sum_{i}v_{i})-1\big).\end{array}

The partial derivatives of
H
are:

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}\displaystyle\frac{\partial H}{\partial v_{i}}(\vec{v},\kappa)&=&\displaystyle\frac{\omega(x_{i})\cdot p(x_{i})}{v_{i}\cdot p(x_{i})}\cdot p(v_{i})-\kappa\hskip 2.0pt=\hskip 2.0pt\displaystyle\frac{\omega(x_{i})\cdot p(x_{i})}{v_{i}}-\kappa\\[8.99994pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\partial H}{\partial\kappa}(\vec{v},\kappa)&=&1-\sum_{i}v_{i}.\end{array}

Setting all these derivatives to zero yields:

\begin{array}[]{rcccccl}1&=&\sum_{i}v_{i}&=&\sum_{i}\displaystyle\frac{\omega(x_{i})\cdot p(x_{i})}{\kappa}&=&\displaystyle\frac{\omega\models p}{\kappa}.\end{array}

Hence
\kappa\,=\,\omega\models p
and thus:

\begin{array}[]{rcccccl}v_{i}&=&\displaystyle\frac{\omega(x_{i})\cdot p(x_{i})}{\kappa}&=&\displaystyle\frac{\omega(x_{i})\cdot p(x_{i})}{\omega\models p}&=&\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}(x_{i}).\end{array}

Hence, Lemma
9
gives as ‘better’
distribution the updated version
\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}
of
\omega
, as in
Theorem
5.1
(
1
), satisfying

\big(\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p$}\models p\big)\geq\big(\omega\models p\big)
.

Proof
(of Theorem
5.1
(
2
))

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{D}(X)
be distribution on a set
X
and let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
be factors on
X
, all with non-zero validity

\omega\models p_{i}
. The claim is that the distribution
\omega^{\prime}=\sum_{i}\frac{1}{n}\cdot\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{i}$}
satisfies:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\displaystyle\prod}_{i}\,(\omega^{\prime}\models p_{i})&\,\geq&{\displaystyle\prod}_{i}\,(\omega\models p_{i}).\end{array}

(36)

We shall prove (
36
) for
n=2
. The
generalisation to arbitrary
n
works similarly, but involves much
more book-keeping of additional variables.

We use Lemma
9
with function
F\colon\mathcal{D}(X)\times X\times X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
given by:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}F(\omega,x,y)&\coloneqq&\omega(x)\cdot p_{1}(x)\cdot\omega(y)\cdot p_{2}(y).\end{array}

Then by distributivity of multiplication over addition:

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}\sum_{x,y}F(\omega,x,y)&=&\big(\sum_{x}\omega(x)\cdot p_{1}(x)\big)\cdot\big(\sum_{y}\omega(y)\cdot p_{2}(y)\big)&=&(\omega\models p_{1})\cdot(\omega\models p_{2}).\end{array}

Let
\mathsl{supp}(\omega)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
and let the
function
H
be given by:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H(\vec{v},\lambda)&\coloneqq&\sum_{i,j}\,F(\omega,x_{i},x_{j})\cdot\ln\big(v_{i}\cdot p_{1}(x_{i})\cdot v_{j}\cdot p_{2}(x_{j})\big)\;-\;\lambda\cdot\big((\sum_{i}v_{i})-1\big).\end{array}

Then:

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}\displaystyle\frac{\partial H}{\partial v_{k}}(\vec{v},\lambda)&=&{\displaystyle\sum}_{i}\,\displaystyle\frac{F(\omega,x_{k},x_{i})+F(\omega,x_{i},x_{k})}{v_{k}}\;-\;\lambda&&\displaystyle\frac{\partial H}{\partial\lambda}(\vec{v},\lambda)&=&1-\sum_{i}v_{i}.\end{array}

Setting these to zero gives:

\begin{array}[]{rcccccl}1&=&\sum_{k}v_{k}&=&\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{k,i}F(\omega,x_{k},x_{i})+F(\omega,x_{i},x_{k})}{\lambda}&=&\displaystyle\frac{2\cdot(\omega\models p_{1})\cdot(\omega\models p_{2})}{\lambda}.\end{array}

Hence
\lambda=2\cdot(\omega\models p_{1})\cdot(\omega\models p_{2})
so that:

\begin{array}[b]{rcl}v_{k}&=&\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{i}F(\omega,x_{k},x_{i})+F(\omega,x_{i},x_{k})}{\lambda}\\[8.00003pt]
&=&\frac{1}{2}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\omega(x_{k})\cdot p_{1}(x_{k})\cdot(\omega\models p_{2})}{(\omega\models p_{1})\cdot(\omega\models p_{2})}+\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{(\omega\models p_{1})\cdot\omega(x_{k})\cdot p_{2}(x_{k})}{(\omega\models p_{1})\cdot(\omega\models p_{2})}\\[8.00003pt]
&=&\frac{1}{2}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\omega(x_{k})\cdot p_{1}(x_{k})}{\omega\models p_{1}}+\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\omega(x_{k})\cdot p_{2}(x_{k})}{\omega\models p_{2}}\\[8.00003pt]
&=&\frac{1}{2}\cdot\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{1}$}(x_{k})+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\omega\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{$|$}\raisebox{-3.01385pt}{$\scriptstyle p_{2}$}(x_{k}).\end{array}
